\displaystyle\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(k_{3}/k_{3})+\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(k_{3}/k_{3})=(2\pi^{3})^{1/2}\mathrm{sech}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(65)

With these two points clarified, we obtain the signal part of 3-point correlator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\text{S}}(u)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi\bigl(\mathrm{i}+\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)}\,}{4\sinh(2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]u^{5/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(66)

Now, we can quote our previous result (
55
) to get the discontinuity of
\mathcal{X}(u)
. After switching to
u=2k_{3}/k_{123}
as the argument, the result reads:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}_{u}{\mathcal{X}}(u)

\displaystyle=-\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}_{k_{12}}\mathcal{I}^{0,-2}(k_{12},k_{3},k_{3})

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\cosh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\mathcal{I}^{0,-2}_{\mathrm{S}}(-k_{12},k_{3},k_{3})\theta(-k_{12}-k_{s})

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\cosh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{S}}\Bigl(\displaystyle\frac{\,{u}\,}{u-1}\Bigr)\theta(-u),

(67)

in which the minus sign in the first line follows from the relation
u=2k_{3}/k_{123}
. Then, from (
66
), we find the discontinuity of the full 3-point correlator as:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}_{u}\mathcal{X}(u)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{4}\bigl(\mathrm{i}-\mathrm{csch}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)\,{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr](-u)^{5/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(68)

Here we have used the following identity to simplify the expression:

\displaystyle{}_{2}\mathrm{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}a,b\\
c\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]=(1-u)^{-a}{}_{2}\mathrm{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}a,c-b\\
c\end{matrix}\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\,{u}\,}{u-1}\biggr].

(69)

Of course, the discontinuity in (
68
) can be directly read from the analytical expression for
\mathcal{X}_{\text{S}}
in (
66
) by using the known analytical properties of hypergeometric function
{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}[\cdots|u]
and the power function
u^{5/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
. However, from (
3.1
), we can check that the discontinuity from the hypergeometric functions get canceled. The net discontinuity (
68
) is fully from the power factor
u^{5/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
, and this will be the key ingredient for our computation of dispersion integral in the next part.

Dispersion integral

With the discontinuity of the function
\mathcal{X}(u)
known, we are ready to form a dispersion integral, which computes the full correlator from its (factorized) discontinuity.

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(u)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{u^{3}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}u^{\prime}}\,}{u^{\prime\,3}(u^{\prime}-u)}\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}_{u^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}(u^{\prime}).

(70)

Here we have introduced a third-order subtraction (
u^{\prime 3}
) to make sure that contour integral vanishes on the large circle. To understand this choice, we note that the large
u
limit of
\mathcal{X}(u)
corresponds to the total-energy limit
k_{123}\rightarrow 0
. By power counting of time, one can see that the seed integral behaves like
\mathcal{X}(u)\sim u^{2}
as
|u|\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, a third-order subtraction suffices to make the dispersion integral well defined.

(
70
) is a well documented integral and can be directly done by
Mathematica
. However, it is instructive to compute this integral more explicitly, by using the partial Mellin-Barnes (PMB) representation
[
37
,
39
]
. This method will be useful for more complicated integrals in the following sections where we do not have readily available integral formulae. Also, as we shall see, there is a nice correspondence between the pole structure of the Mellin integral and the analytical property of the final result. (See Fig.
6
).

\Rightarrow

Figure 6:
The dispersion integrals for the 3-point correlator. The left panel shows the integral (
70
) on the complex
u^{\prime}
plane, where we have a branch cut of the 3-point correlator in the unphysical region
u^{\prime}\in(-\infty,0)
, and an inserted pole at the physical point
u^{\prime}=u
with
0<u<1
. On the right panel, we show the corresponding Mellin integral (
3.1
) on the complex plane of Mellin variable
s
. The blue and red poles on the left side of the integration contour contribute to the signal and the background of the full 3-point correlator, respectively.

To apply the PMB technique, we use the following MB representation for the hypergeometric function:
5
5

5

Generally, there is certain flexibility to deform the integral contour, so long as all poles coming from “
\Gamma(+as+\cdots)
are to the left of the contour, and those poles from “
\Gamma(-bs+\cdots)
” are to the right. (Here
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.) For convenience, here we just label the lower/upper bound of the integral as
\mp\mathrm{i}\infty
.

\displaystyle{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1\mp 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u^{\prime}\biggr]=\int_{-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{\mathrm{i}\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\ (-u^{\prime})^{-s}\Gamma\biggl[\begin{matrix}s,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-s\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-s\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1-s\mp 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\biggr].

(71)

We see that the MB representation effectively turns a complicated dependence on
u^{\prime}
into a simple power function
(-u^{\prime})^{-s}
. As a result, the dispersion integral over
u^{\prime}
in (
70
) can now be trivially carried out as:

\displaystyle\frac{\,{u^{3}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}u^{\prime}\,(-u^{\prime})^{5/2\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu-s}}\,}{u^{\prime\,3}(u^{\prime}-u)}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\sec\big[\pi(s\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\big]}\,}{2\mathrm{i}}u^{5/2-s\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}.

(72)

It then remains to finish the Mellin integral over
s
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(u)=

\displaystyle\ \int_{-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{\mathrm{i}\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{u^{5/2-s-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\sec\big[{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\pi(s+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\big]\Gamma\biggl[\begin{matrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s},\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-s-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-s-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1-s-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\biggr]

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{8}\bigl(1+\mathrm{i}\ \mathrm{csch}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(73)

Since we have taken the variable
u
in the physical region, i.e.,
u\in(0,1)
, we can perform the above Mellin integral by closing the contour from the left side. The integrand decreases fast enough when
s
goes to infinity in the left plane, so that the integral over the large semi-circle on the left plane vanishes, and we can finish the integral by collecting the residues of all poles to the left side of the original integration contour. From (
3.1
), we see that there are two sets of left poles contributing to the final results, whose origins are highlighted in red and blue colors:
6
6

6

When computing integrals via PMB representation, if a Gamma function contributes poles, then all of its poles need to be collected. For example, here there are a set of poles from
\Gamma[s]
, then we need to pick up the whole set of these poles, i.e.,
s=-n
where
n=0,1,2,\cdots
. The case where poles come from
\sec[\pi(s+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)]
is a little subtler: If we change the upper bound of the integral over
u^{\prime}
from
0
to
-\epsilon
where
\epsilon
is a small positive real number, one can find we will get
\Gamma[1/2+s+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]
instead of
\sec[\pi(s+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)]
. It is only in the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
that the Gamma function
\Gamma[1/2+s+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]
will meet another Gamma function
\Gamma[1/2-s-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]
and give rise to
\sec[\pi(s+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)]
. This implies when considering poles from
\sec[\pi(s+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)]
we actually need to collect all poles from
\Gamma[1/2+s+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]
, while poles from
\Gamma[1/2-s-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]
should be omitted. This analysis gives us another set of poles, i.e.,
s=-1/2-n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
where
n=0,1,2,\cdots
.

\left\{\begin{aligned} &{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}s=-\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu};\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}s=-n}.~~~~(n=0,1,2,\cdots)\end{aligned}\right.

(74)

We also show these poles in the right panel of Fig.
6
.
Clearly, from the factor
u^{5/2-s-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
in the integrand in (
3.1
), we see that the poles
s=-1/2-n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
correspond to the background, whose residues sum to:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\text{B}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigl(1+\mathrm{i}\ \mathrm{csch}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{n}}\,}{8}u^{3+n}\Gamma\biggl[\begin{matrix}1+n,3+n,-\frac{1}{2}-n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
\frac{3}{2}+n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\biggr]+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\frac{\,{2u^{3}}\,}{1+4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}{}_{3}\mathrm{F}_{2}\biggl[\begin{matrix}1,1,3\\
\frac{3}{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\frac{3}{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr].

(75)

On the other hand, the poles at
s=-n
in the integrand of (
3.1
) give rise to the signal:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\text{S}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{sech}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigl(1+\mathrm{i}\ \mathrm{csch}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{8}u^{5/2+n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Gamma\biggl[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}+n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\frac{5}{2}+n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1+n,1+n-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\biggr]+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi\bigl(\mathrm{i}+\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)}\,}{4\sinh(2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]u^{5/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(76)

Thus, the whole three-point correlator
{\mathcal{X}}(u)
is neatly expressed as a sum of the signal and the background:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(u)=

\displaystyle~\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi\bigl(\mathrm{i}+\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)}\,}{4\sinh(2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]u^{5/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg\}

\displaystyle~-\displaystyle\frac{\,{2u^{3}}\,}{1+4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}\,{}_{3}\mathrm{F}_{2}\biggl[\begin{matrix}1,1,3\\
\frac{3}{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,\frac{3}{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr].

(77)

This agrees with the results found previously using a different method
[
46
]
.

To recapitulate our strategy, the PMB representation converts special functions into simple power functions, making the dispersion integrals easier to compute. Thereafter, the integration over Mellin variables can be directly computed via residue theorem. Therefore, the PMB representation provides a convenient way to calculate dispersion integrals analytically. For inflation correlators more complicated than the one considered here, the PMB representation remains useful, and will be shown below.

3.2
Three-point one-loop bubble graph

Figure 7:
The 3-point correlator with 1-loop massive scalar exchange. For simplicity, we take the two bulk-to-bulk propagators to have the same mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
.

Although most of the discussions of this work focus on tree-level processes, the dispersion technique can also be applied to loop processes. In this subsection, we will explore a simple 1-loop diagram with dispersion relations, with the help of the technique of spectral decomposition
[
42
]
. Our example comes from the following interactions between the massless scalar
\varphi
and the principal scalar
\sigma
:

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathscr{L}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\kappa_{4}}\,}{4}a^{2}\varphi^{\prime 2}\sigma^{2}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{\kappa_{3}}\,}{2}a^{3}\varphi^{\prime}\sigma^{2}.

(78)

Then, at
\mathcal{O}({\kappa_{4}\kappa_{3}})
, there is a unique diagram (up to trivial permutations) contributing to the 3-point correlator of
\varphi
with a bubble loop formed by
\sigma
; See Fig.
7
.

Similar to the tree-level case, we can extract a dimensionless seed integral from the correlator:

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{\bm{k}_{1}}\varphi_{\bm{k}_{2}}\varphi_{\bm{k}_{3}}\rangle^{\prime}_{\text{1-loop}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\kappa_{3}\kappa_{4}}\,}{8k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}^{4}}\bigg[{\mathcal{J}}^{0,-2}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{2k_{3}}\,}{k_{123}}\Big)+\text{2 perms}\bigg].

(79)

Here
\mathcal{J}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
is the corresponding seed integral, defined as a function of the momentum ratio
u=2k_{3}/k_{123}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(u)\equiv-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{a,b}=\pm}\mathsf{ab}\ k_{3}^{5+p_{12}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}(-\tau_{1})^{p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{p_{2}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{3}\tau_{2}}\mathbf{Q}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}_{\mathsf{ab}}(k_{3};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(80)

Here,
\mathbf{Q}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}_{\mathsf{ab}}
denotes the 3-momentum loop integral:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}_{\mathsf{ab}}(k_{3};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\equiv\int\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}^{3}\bm{q}}\,}{(2\pi)^{3}}D^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}_{\mathsf{ab}}\Bigl(q;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\Bigr)D^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}_{\mathsf{ab}}\Bigl(|\bm{k}_{3}-\bm{q}|;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\Bigr).

(81)

Here we mark out the mass parameter
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
of propagators as
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
is important in the following analysis.

As explained in previous works
[
169
,
42
,
52
]
, the loop integral (
81
) can be recast as a (continuous) linear superposition of massive propagators
{D}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{ab}}
with different values of
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
, weighted by a spectral function
\rho^{\mathrm{dS}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}_{\mathsf{ab}}(k_{3};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\int_{-\infty-\mathrm{i}\epsilon}^{+\infty+\mathrm{i}\epsilon}{\mathrm{d}}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}}\,}{\pi\mathrm{i}}\rho^{\mathrm{dS}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})D^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})}_{\mathsf{ab}}(k_{3};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(82)

With the assumption that both the time integrals in (
80
) and the spectral integral in (
82
) are convergent,
7
7

7

The convergence of the spectral integral (
82
) requires a proper regularization procedure, such as dimensional regularization, to make the spectral function
\rho^{\mathrm{dS}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})
finite in the first place. However, as we will see below, our treatment of the loop process is completely independent of the regularization, and we can safely stay in
d=3
throughout the discussion.
we can switch the order of two integrals, and write the loop correlator
\mathcal{J}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
as a spectral integral over tree correlator
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(u)=\int_{-\infty-\mathrm{i}\epsilon}^{+\infty+\mathrm{i}\epsilon}{\mathrm{d}}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\rho^{\mathrm{dS}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime})\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}}(k_{12},k_{3},k_{3}).

(83)

Now we specialize to the case of
(p_{1},p_{2})=(0,-2)
as indicated in (
79
), and form a dispersion integral for
\mathcal{J}^{0,-2}
. Such a dispersion integral is possible, because all the 3-point tree-level correlators
\mathcal{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}}^{0,-2}
with different mass parameters
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}
satisfy the same dispersion relation (
70
). Therefore, their linear superposition in (
83
) should satisfies a similar dispersion integral. However, we should expect that the subtraction order for the loop correlator differs from the tree due to the different UV behavior. Therefore, let us write down the dispersion integral for the loop seed integral
\mathcal{J}^{0,-2}(u)
on
u
plane in the following way:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}^{0,-2}(u)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{u^{m}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}u^{\prime}}\,}{u^{\prime m}(u^{\prime}-u)}\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}_{u^{\prime}}{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathrm{S}}^{0,-2}(u^{\prime}).

(84)

Here we leave the subtraction order
m
arbitrary, and we will determine it later.

As mentioned above, the loop seed integral
\mathcal{J}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
has been computed purely from spectral decomposition in
[
42
]
. However, the result in
[
42
]
shows a significant hierarchy in the degree of complication between the signal and the background: The signal part of the loop diagram is a discrete sum of tree signals weighted by a simple coefficients, which can be understood as summing over all quasinormal modes of the loop. On the other hand, the background part is quite complicated, which, after regularization and renormalization, contains a highly intricate special function in the renormalized spectral function. Below, we shall exploit this hierarchy, using the signal computed via the spectral decomposition to bootstrap the full correlator, and thus bypassing any complications of regularization and renormalization.

Thus, our starting point will be the signal part of the loop seed integral computed via the spectral decomposition
[
42
]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{0,-2}_{\text{S}}(u)=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\frac{\,{u^{4+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{128\pi\sin(-2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(3+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+4n)(1+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}\,}{(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n,4+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n\\
4+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+4n\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]u^{2n}+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(85)

We then need to get the discontinuity of the signal along the branch cut. For the signal (
3.2
), its discontinuity along the branch cut
u\in(-\infty,0)
is simply contributed by the
u^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
factor, which is similar to the tree-level case.
The result is

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}_{u}{\mathcal{J}}^{0,-2}_{\mathrm{S}}(u)=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\frac{\,{(-u)^{4+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{64\pi\mathrm{i}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(3+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+4n)(1+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}\,}{(\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(\frac{3}{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n,4+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n\\
4+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+4n\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]u^{2n}\theta(-u)+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(86)

Now we are ready to use (
84
) and (
3.2
) to compute the full correlator. However, at this point, we need to choose a subtraction (namely, to choose a value of
m
in (
84
)) to make sure that the integral (
84
) converges when
u\rightarrow 0
and
u\rightarrow-\infty
. Examining the behavior of the integrand in these two limits, we see that the convergence as
u\rightarrow 0
requires
m\leq 4
while the convergence as
u\rightarrow-\infty
prefers a large
m
. So,
m=4
is an optimal choice.

Similar to the 3-point tree-level case, for every term in (
3.2
), the dispersion integral can be done either by
Mathematica
directly or by PMB representation. The final result for the loop seed integral
\mathcal{J}^{0,-2}(u)
is again the sum of the signal
\mathcal{J}_{\text{S}}^{0,-2}(u)
and the background
\mathcal{J}^{0,-2}_{\text{BG}}(u)
. The signal is already given in (
3.2
), and the background is given by:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}^{0,-2}_{\mathrm{BG}}(u)=\

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{u^{4}}\,}{128\pi\sin(2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(3+4n+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)(1+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(1+n+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)_{\frac{1}{2}}}\,}{(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}+n+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+n+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times{}_{3}{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\biggl[\begin{matrix}1,2,4\\
1-2n-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,4+2n+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(87)

Here
{}_{3}\mathcal{F}_{2}
is the dressed version of the generalized hypergeometric function, whose definition is collected in App.
B
.

Some readers may find it mysterious that no UV divergence ever shows up in our calculation.
The reason is in fact clear: The UV divergence in this 1-loop correlator can be fully subtracted by a local counterterm
{\mathscr{L}}\supset\delta_{\lambda}a\varphi^{\prime 3}
with divergent coefficient
\delta_{\lambda}
. At the correlator level, this counterterm produces a contact diagram
\propto\delta_{\lambda}u^{3}
, and thus is analytical on the entire
u
plane. If we follow the standard loop calculation, we would find a divergent part proportional to
u^{3}
, plus a finite part with more complicated
u
dependence. Then we can use any convenient regularization method to remove the divergence, and use any proper renormalization scheme to determine the finite coefficient of the
u^{3}
term. The arbitrariness of the coefficient of the
u^{3}
term is an intrinsic uncertainty of the loop calculation.

We think that this is an important lesson, especially for readers not very familiar with loop calculations, so let us reiterate it: When computing a superficially UV divergent loop correlator, the UV divergence is simply an artifact of our computation method and unphysical. Therefore, we may find a method so that UV divergences never appear and we never need to do UV regularization. Indeed, our dispersion method here is such an example where the regularization is never needed.

On the contrary, when computing a 1-loop correlator with whatever methods, the result may contain a finite number of terms (in our case, the number is 1), whose kinematic dependence is totally fixed but coefficient undetermined. Indeed, the kinematic dependences of these terms are simply given by the corresponding tree graphs from the local counterterm in ordinary calculations, while the coefficients of these terms are never fixed by computation only; Instead, they should be determined by a renormalization condition, or, in a loose sense, by experimental data. Thus, to summarize: in a UV-divergent loop correlator, the UV divergence may be avoidable, but the renormalization ambiguity is not avoidable.

For readers familiar with flat-space loop calculations with dimensional regularization, in App.
E
, we provide a direct comparison between our dispersive calculation and the more conventional computation for a Minkowski 1-loop correlator, where one can see explicitly that the dispersion integral itself is free of any UV divergence or renormalization dependence, and that all renormalization-dependent information is fully encoded in the subtraction point.

Back to our dispersion method, it is now clear that the renormalization ambiguity cannot be probed by the nonanalyticity of the correlator, and therefore we are not going to recover them from a dispersion integral. What we did recover in (
3.2
), therefore, is a background free of any UV ambiguity, whose existence is demanded by analyticity of the correlator. For this reason, we call it the
irreducible background
.

The physical meaning of this irreducible background is clear: For the loop diagram in question, we can imagine to integrate out all loop modes and get infinitely many effective 3-point self-interaction vertices of the external mode, with increasing number of derivatives. These derivative couplings contribute to the 3-point function in the form of a Taylor expansion of
u
, starting from
u^{3}
. Except the renormalization-dependent term
\propto u^{3}
, all terms starting from
\mathcal{O}({u^{4}})
are UV free and unambiguously determined by the loop computation. They can still be treated as from local (albeit derivative) interactions, but the coefficients of these interactions are unambiguous prediction of the model. Our result for the background (
3.2
) precisely recovers these terms.

With the above remark on renormalization ambiguity in mind, we can summarize our result for the loop seed integral as:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}^{0,-2}(u)=Cu^{3}-\displaystyle\frac{\,{u^{4}}\,}{128\pi\sin(2\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(3+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+4n)(1+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(1+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}\,}{(\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+n)_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}2+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n,4+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+2n\\
4+4\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+4n\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]u^{2n+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}-{}_{3}{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\biggl[\begin{matrix}1,2,4\\
1-2n-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,4+2n+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|u\biggr]\bigg\}

\displaystyle+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(88)

Here the first term
Cu^{3}
is a local term, whose coefficient
C
is to be determined by a renormalization scheme. The rest of terms, including the signal and the irreducible background, are free from renormalization ambiguities. They are both organized as an infinite summation over quasi-normal modes of the bubble loop.

Although it is difficult to analytically compare our result (
3.2
) with the known background obtained from the spectral decomposition in
[
42
]
, we find that their numerical results only differ by a
u^{3}
-term, which is exactly the undetermined local part
Cu^{3}
in (
3.2
). Given the very complicated form of the background in
[
42
]
, we consider this agreement a rather nontrivial check of both methods.

3.3
Four-point single-exchange graph

Figure 8:
The analytical structure of the 4-point seed integral
\mathcal{Y}(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2})
on the complex
r_{1}^{\prime}
plane, with
r_{2}\in(0,1)
fixed in the interior of its physical region. There are three branch points at
r_{1}^{\prime}=0
(signal branch point),
r_{1}^{\prime}=-r_{2}
(total-energy pole), and
r_{1}^{\prime}=-1
(partial-energy pole). with a branch cut connecting them in the interval
r_{1}^{\prime}\in(-1,0)
. In this plot, we also show the insertion of a pole at
r_{1}^{\prime}=r_{1}
at which we compute the seed integral via the dispersion integral (
3.3
), and the blue circle surrounding the branch cut is the contour for the dispersion integral.

As our last application of the vertex dispersion relation, we return to the 4-point seed integral (
11
). Once again, we work with a particular choice of the exponents
(p_{1},p_{2})=(-2,-2)
. As explained in (
12
), this corresponds to the case of nonderivative coupling
\phi_{c}^{2}\sigma
between the conformal scalar
\phi_{c}
in the external legs and a general principal massive scalar
\sigma
in the bulk line.

Similar to the previous 3-point examples, we want to exploit the scale invariance of the process, which implies that the the seed integral
\mathcal{I}^{-2,-2}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
depends on three energy variables only through two independent momentum ratios. For the 4-point case, it is convenient to choose the following pair of ratios:

\displaystyle r_{1}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{k_{s}}\,}{k_{12}},

\displaystyle r_{2}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{k_{s}}\,}{k_{34}}.

(89)

The physical region
0\leq k_{s}\leq\text{min}\{k_{12},k_{34}\}
then corresponds to
r_{1,2}\in[0,1]
.
We then translate the analytical structure of the seed integral on the complex
k_{12}
plane (Fig.
4
) to the complex
r_{1}
plane, keeping
r_{2}\in(0,1)
staying in the interior of the physical region. We show the result in Fig.
8
, where the total-energy pole
k_{12}=-k_{34}
, the partial energy pole
k_{12}=-k_{s}
, and the signal branch point
k_{12}\rightarrow-\infty
correspond to
r_{1}=-r_{2}
,
r_{1}=-1
, and
r_{1}=0
, respectively. Also, the branch cut is now entirely moved to the interval
r_{1}\in(-1,0)
.

To highlight that we are working with
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
as arguments of the seed integral, we use a new notation
\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})
for the 4-point seed integral:

\mathcal{Y}(r_{1}=k_{s}/k_{12},r_{2}=k_{s}/k_{34})\equiv\mathcal{I}^{-2,-2}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s}).

(90)

Then, from (
2.4
), we can read the signal
\mathcal{Y}_{\text{S}}(r_{1},r_{2})\equiv\mathcal{I}_{\text{S}}^{-2,-2}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
of the seed integral, which is responsible for all the discontinuities:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\text{S}}(r_{1},r_{2})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Y}_{\text{S},>}(r_{1},r_{2})\theta(r_{2}-r_{1})+\mathcal{Y}_{\text{S},<}(r_{1},r_{2})\theta(r_{1}-r_{2});

(91)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\text{S},>}(r_{1},r_{2})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{I}^{-2,-2}_{+-}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})+\mathcal{I}^{-2,-2}_{++,\mathrm{F},>}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})+\mathrm{c.c.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{4}e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big[{\mathcal{U}}_{+}^{-2}(k_{12},k_{s}){\mathcal{U}}_{-}^{-2}(k_{34},k_{s})-{\mathcal{U}}_{-}^{-2}(e^{\mathrm{i}\pi}k_{12},k_{s}){\mathcal{U}}_{+}^{-2}(k_{34},k_{s})\Big]+\text{c.c.};

(92)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{S},<}(r_{1},r_{2})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{S},>}(r_{2},r_{1}).

(93)

Using the expressions for
\mathcal{U}^{p}_{\pm}
in (
62
), we can find the explicit result for the signal:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{S},>}(r_{1},r_{2})=\Bigl(\displaystyle\frac{\,{1-\mathrm{i}\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{2\pi}r_{1}^{1/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Bigr)\Bigl(r_{2}^{1/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{2})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Bigr),

(94)

where
\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{p}
is defined in (
63
). Then, with
r_{2}\in(0,1)
fixed in the interior of the physical region, the discontinuity of the seed function
\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})
on the real axis of
r_{1}
is itself a piecewise function of
r_{1}
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})=2\mathrm{i}\cosh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{S}}(-r_{1},r_{2})\theta(-r_{1})\theta(r_{1}+1)

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\cosh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Bigl[\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{S},>}(-r_{1},r_{2})\theta(r_{1}+r_{2})\theta(-r_{1})+\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{S},<}(-r_{1},r_{2})\theta(r_{1}+1)\theta(-r_{1}-r_{2})\Bigr].

(95)

This result is derived directly from (
55
), although there is a sign difference in
\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}\limits_{r_{1}}\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}\limits_{k_{12}}\mathcal{I}^{-2,-2}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
due to the relation
r_{1}=k_{s}/k_{12}
.
Since the seed integral
\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})
is regular when
|r_{1}|\rightarrow\infty
and
r_{2}
fixed at a finite point, we can directly construct a dispersion integral for
\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})
from (
3.3
), with a first-order subtraction to ensure the vanishing integral along the large circle:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-1}^{0}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}r}\,}{r(r-r_{1})}\mathop{\mathrm{Disc}}_{r}\mathcal{Y}(r,r_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\cosh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{\pi}\bigg[\int_{-r_{2}}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}r\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{S},>}(-r,r_{2})}\,}{r(r-r_{1})}+\int_{-1}^{-r_{2}}{\mathrm{d}}r\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{S},<}(-r,r_{2})}\,}{r(r-r_{1})}\bigg],

(96)

With the explicit expressions for the signal
\mathcal{Y}_{\text{S}}
in (
94
) and (
93
), the dispersion integral (
3.3
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})=\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{\,{1-\mathrm{i}\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{2\pi^{2}}\cosh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu){r_{1}}\mathbf{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(r_{1},r_{2})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg]\bigg[r_{2}^{1/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{2})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg]

\displaystyle+\bigg[{r_{1}}\mathbf{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(r_{1},r_{2})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg]\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{\,{1-\mathrm{i}\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{2\pi^{2}}\cosh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)r_{2}^{1/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{2})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigg],

(97)

where
\mathbf{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}
are the two integrals that are derived from the vertex dispersion relation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(r_{1},r_{2})\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{-r_{2}}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}r\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-r)^{1/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(-r)}\,}{r(r-r_{1})},

(98)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(r_{1},r_{2})\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{-r_{2}}{\mathrm{d}}r\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-r)^{1/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(-r)}\,}{r(r-r_{1})}.

(99)

Unlike the 3-point case where the integrals extend from
-\infty
to 0, the integrals here are defined on finite intervals
(-r_{2},0)
and
(-1,-r_{2})
, making the calculation more involved. Still, we can get their analytical results by using the PMB representation, although the actual computation is quite lengthy. We collect the main steps and the final results for these two integrals in App.
C
.

Once
\mathbf{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{I}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}
are obtained, we get the full expression of the seed integral
\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})
, which can be further simplified and separated into the signal and the background, namely,
\mathcal{Y}(r_{1},r_{2})=\mathcal{Y}_{\text{S}}(r_{1},r_{2})+\mathcal{Y}_{\text{BG}}(r_{1},r_{2})
.
The simplification is spelled out in App.
C
. Here, we only show the final result for the background
\mathcal{Y}_{\text{BG}}(r_{1},r_{2})
, since the signal
\mathcal{Y}_{\text{S}}(r_{1},r_{2})
has been given in (
91
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\text{BG}}(r_{1},r_{2})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Y}_{\text{BG},>}(r_{1},r_{2})\theta(r_{1}-r_{2})+\mathcal{Y}_{\text{BG},>}(r_{2},r_{1})\theta(r_{2}-r_{1});

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\mathrm{BG},>}(r_{1},r_{2})=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}2^{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\coth(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1+n-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)_{n-1/2}}\,}{n!}{\,}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}1,\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}-2n+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\\
\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}-2n+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|-\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{r_{2}}\biggr]

\displaystyle\times{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{4}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$},\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{4}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\\
1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\bigg|r_{2}^{2}\biggr]r_{1}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{2}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2n}+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(100)

This expression appears different from the known results in the literature
[
39
]
, but a direct numerical check shows that they agree with each other. Therefore, we have successfully bootstrapped the 4-point correlation functions with single massive exchange by dispersion integrals.

As we can see, for this particular 4-point example, performing the dispersion integral is by no means simpler than performing the nest time integral directly
[
39
]
. Rather, our calculation here serves as a proof of principle, and shows that the dispersion relations really work for correlators with more complicated kinematics than 3-point single-exchange diagram. On the other hand, we can anticipate that the use of dispersion relation can bring significant simplification to the 4-point correlators at 1-loop level. We will pursue this 1-loop calculation in a future work.

4  Analytical Structure on the Complex Line-Energy Plane

In the previous two sections, we considered the analytical properties and dispersion relations of inflation correlators in the complex plane of a vertex energy. Starting from this section, we are going to study the analytical properties of inflation correlators from a different perspective, by treating a line energy as a complex variable. In general, inflation correlators with massive exchanges also develop branch cuts on the complex plane of line energies. Therefore, it is possible to develop a different type of dispersion relations on the line energy plane, which we call
line dispersion relations
. As we shall show, branch cuts on the complex plane of a line energy can all be connected to the
nonlocal signal
of the inflation correlator. Therefore, a line dispersion relation allows us to compute the entire inflation correlator from its nonlocal signal alone.

At the first sight, it may appear trivial that the branch cuts on a line energy plane can be entirely attributed to the nonlocal signal. Indeed, recall that the nonlocal signal with respect to a line energy
K_{i}
refers to the part of the correlator which develops complex powers in
K_{i}
in the soft
K_{i}
limit:

\lim_{K_{i}\rightarrow 0}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\Big(\{E_{\ell}\},\{K_{j}\}\Big)\sim f\Big(\{E_{\ell}\},\{K_{j}\}\Big)K_{i}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}+g\Big(\{E_{\ell}\},\{K_{j}\}\Big),

(101)

where both
f
and
g
are analytic at
K_{i}=0
, i.e., they have ordinary Taylor expansions at
K_{i}=0
. Therefore, the nonlocal signal, by definition, is associated with the branch point at
K=0
generated by the complex-power term
f(\{E_{\ell}\},\{K_{j}\})K_{i}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
. However, things are less trivial than they appear: The functions
f
and
g
are analytic in
K_{i}
only within a finite domain around
K_{i}=0
where their Taylor expansions converge. Outside the convergence domain, these two functions could well develop new nonanalytic behaviors, including branch cuts, on the
K_{i}
plane. These new nonanalyticities, in particular the ones in
g
, are not obviously related to the nonlocal signal. Therefore, it is quite remarkable that all branch cuts on the
K_{i}
plane, including those not generated by nonlocal signals, can actually be connected to the nonlocal signal alone. In this section, we will spell out the details of reducing the entire correlator to its nonlocal signal. In this sense, we may say that the nonlocal signal by itself knows all about the whole correlator.

Recall from the previous two sections that a vertex dispersion integral relates an inflation correlator with its
signal
, both local and nonlocal. On the other hand, the line dispersion enables the recovery of full correlator from the nonlocal signal alone. Therefore, we see that the line dispersion is more “economic” than vertex dispersion in that it can generate the full correlator from a smaller set of data. This may have a practical advantage for bootstrapping inflation correlators, since the nonlocal signal appears easier to identify and to compute than the local signal, especially at the loop level
[
37
,
49
,
50
]
. Therefore, we may expect that the line dispersion relation may be a useful tool to bootstrap some complicated loop correlators whose full analytical results remain out of reach with currently known methods.

Defining the nonlocal signal

Clearly, the nonlocal signal plays a central role in the line dispersion relation. By definition, the nonlocal signal is a term in the correlator that develops complex powers
K^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
in the soft line energy limit
K\rightarrow 0
, namely the
f(E_{\ell},K_{j})K_{i}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
term in (
101
). Now let us identify this piece in the four-point seed integral
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
in (
11
) without really computing it.

When we fix the two vertex energies
k_{12}
and
k_{34}
in their physical domain and let
k_{s}\rightarrow 0
, the seed integral
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
is well convergent in the early time limit. Thus, its analytical behavior at
k_{s}=0
is fully determined by the analytical property of the integrand in
k_{s}
, which in turn is determined by the bulk propagator
D_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k_{s};\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
. Clearly, all four bulk propagators listed in (
4
)-(
6
) are constructed from a pair of Hankel functions
\mathrm{H}_{+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}
and
\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}
. Thus, we can regroup these Hankel functions to separate all bulk propagators into a piece analytic at
k_{s}=0
and a piece that contains complex powers in
k_{s}
. In practice, this can be neatly done by rewriting each Hankel function as a linear combination of Bessel function of the first kind
\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
; See (
159
). Then, the Hankel product in the propagator can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}

{}^{(2)}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathrm{csch}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)^{2}\mathrm{J}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{J}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{2})+\bigl(1+\coth(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)^{2}\mathrm{J}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{J}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{2})

\displaystyle-\mathrm{csch}(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigl(1+\coth(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\bigr)\Big[\mathrm{J}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{J}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{2})+\mathrm{J}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{J}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k_{s}\tau_{2})\Big].

(102)

In this expression, we have two types of terms: One involves a product of two
\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
with opposite orders, namely
\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathrm{J}_{\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
, as listed in the first line on the right hand side of (
4
); The other type involves a product of two
\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
with the same order, namely
\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
, listed in the last line of (
4
). By expanding these Bessel J functions in the
k_{s}\rightarrow 0
limit, it is straightforward to see that the opposite-order terms
\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathrm{J}_{\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
are analytic as
k_{s}\rightarrow 0
, while the same-order terms
\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
behaves like
k_{s}^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
as
k_{s}\rightarrow 0
. Thus, the same-order terms in the propagators precisely give rise to the nonlocal-signal part of the seed integral, while the opposite-order terms contain no nonlocal signal. Either by more careful inspection of the integral or by direct calculation, one can confirm that the opposite-order terms correspond to the local signal and the background, but we will not need this detailed separation between the local signal and the background in this section. Incidentally, from the boundary viewpoint, the same-order part can be viewed as the two-point correlator of a given conformal block with dimension
\Delta=\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
, while the opposite-order part the correlator between a conformal block and its shadow.

Based on the above observation, we now separate all four bulk propagators
D_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
according to their analytic property at
k\rightarrow 0
in the following way:

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\Sigma(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})+\Omega_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(103)

Here the
same-order propagators

\Sigma(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
involve terms with same-order Bessel-J products, and thus are nonanalytic at
k=0
:

\displaystyle\Sigma(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\equiv

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{3/2}}\,}{4\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\Big[\mathrm{J}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k\tau_{1})\mathrm{J}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k\tau_{2})+\mathrm{J}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k\tau_{1})\mathrm{J}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k\tau_{2})\Big],

(104)

while the four
opposite-order propagators

\Omega_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
involve terms with opposite-order Bessel-J products, and thus are analytic at
k=0
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\pm\mp}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\equiv

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{3/2}}\,}{4\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\Big[\big(\coth(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)-1\big)\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k\tau_{1})\mathrm{J}_{\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-k\tau_{2})-(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Big],

\displaystyle\Omega_{\pm\pm}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\equiv

\displaystyle~\Omega_{\mp\pm}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})+\Omega_{\mp\pm}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}).

(105)

We have deliberately removed the SK indices in the same-order propagators
\Sigma(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, to highlight the fact that this propagator is actually independent of the SK contours: All four choices of the SK labels
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}=\pm
yield the same expression
\Sigma(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
. This is closely tied to the fact that the nonanalytic part of the propagator is real and symmetric in the two time variables
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
. In particular, the symmetry under
\tau_{1}\leftrightarrow\tau_{2}
renders the time-ordering
\theta
functions ineffective in the same-sign propagators.

However, let us immediately clarify that the same-order propagator
\Sigma(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
is
not
the symmetrization of the original bulk propagator
D_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
with respect to
\tau_{1}\leftrightarrow r_{2}
. As one can directly check, the opposite-order propagator
\Omega_{\pm\pm}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
also contains a piece that is symmetric with respect to
\tau_{1}\leftrightarrow r_{2}
but is nevertheless analytic at
k_{s}=0
. In fact, this additional piece corresponds to a part of the local signal that is symmetric in
k_{12}\leftrightarrow k_{34}
.

Now, we can put the above separated bulk propagator back into the seed integral, and separate the seed integral accordingly:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})+\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s}),

(106)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
are respectively nonanalytic and analytic at
k_{s}\rightarrow 0
when
k_{12}
and
k_{34}
staying in the interior of their physical domain, whose definitions are:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})\equiv

\displaystyle-\mathsf{ab}\ k_{s}^{5+p_{12}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\,(-\tau_{1})^{p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{p_{2}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{34}\tau_{2}}\Sigma(k_{s};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),

(107)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})\equiv

\displaystyle-\mathsf{ab}\ k_{s}^{5+p_{12}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\,(-\tau_{1})^{p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{p_{2}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{34}\tau_{2}}\Omega_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k_{s};\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(108)

We note that, although the same-order propagator itself
\Sigma(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
is independent of SK indices, the nonanalytic integrals
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
still have nontrivial dependences on
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}=\pm
through the exponential factors
e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{b}k_{34}\tau_{2}}
.

To complete our list of new definitions, we can also define the integrals with SK indices summed:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})\equiv\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}=\pm}\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s});

(109)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})\equiv\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}=\pm}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s}).

(110)

From the above discussion, we see that
\mathcal{P}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
is nothing but the nonlocal signal, while
\mathcal{Q}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
is the sum of the local signal and the background:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s}),

(111)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{I}_{\text{LS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})+\mathcal{I}_{\text{BG}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s}).

(112)

Same-order integral

Now let us briefly look at the two integrals defined in (
109
) and (
110
). First, consider the same-order integral
\mathcal{P}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
. Combining (
104
), (
107
), and (
109
), we see that the nonlocal signal can be directly expressed as a sum of factorized time integrals:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{4\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{c}=\pm}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{c}}^{p_{1}}(\mathsf{a}k_{12},k_{s})\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{c}}^{p_{2}}(\mathsf{b}k_{34},k_{s}),

(113)

where we have introduced two single-layer integrals
\mathcal{V}_{\pm}^{p}(E,K)
, defined by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\pm}^{p}(E,K)\equiv

\displaystyle~K^{5/2+p}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\,(-\tau)^{3/2+p}e^{+\mathrm{i}E\tau}\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-K\tau).

(114)

This integral can be directly done and the result is expressed in terms of the (dressed) Gauss’s hypergeometric function: (See App.
B
for our definition of the dressed hypergeometric functions.)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\pm}^{p}(E,K)=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{2^{3/2+p}}\,}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K}\,}{\mathrm{i}E}\Big)^{5/2+p\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}+p\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu),\mbox{$\frac{\,{1}\,}{2}$}(\mbox{$\frac{\,{7}\,}{2}$}+p\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\\
1\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\middle|\displaystyle\frac{\,{K^{2}}\,}{E^{2}}\right].

(115)

Thus, the computation of the nonlocal signal involves only single-layer integrals, which is a direct consequence of the nonlocal-signal cutting rule studied in the literature
[
33
,
49
,
50
]
.

Parity of the opposite-order integral

Next, let us turn to the opposite-order integrals
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
. Unlike the nonlocal signal, these integrals involve genuine time orderings that cannot be removed, resulting in final expressions of higher “transcendental weight”
[
67
]
, and thus are more difficult to compute. We are going to compute them using dispersion relations below. Here, without computing them directly, we point out that the integral
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
has a very useful property: It possesses a fixed parity under the parity transformation of the line energy:
k_{s}\rightarrow-k_{s}
.

To see this point, we make use of a property of Bessel J function, given in App.
B
, which shows that
\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(e^{\mathrm{i}\pi}z)\mathrm{J}_{\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(e^{\mathrm{i}\pi}w)=\mathrm{J}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(z)\mathrm{J}_{\mp\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(w)
. As a result, the opposite-order propagator
\Omega_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
is invariant under the sign flip of its energy:

\Omega_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(-k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\Omega_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(116)

With this property and the definition of the opposite-order integral in (
108
), it is straightforward to see that
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
has a fixed parity
(-1)^{1+p_{12}}
under the
k_{s}
-parity transformation
k_{s}\rightarrow-k_{s}
:

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},-k_{s})=(-1)^{1+p_{12}}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s}),

(117)

where
(-1)^{1+p_{12}}
comes entirely from the prefactor
k_{s}^{5+p_{12}}
in our definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
. This property will be very useful for our following derivation of the line dispersion relation.

Analyticity along the positive real axis

After a brief analysis of same-order and opposite-order integrals, now let us come back to the main goal of this section, namely, to diagnose the nonanalyticity of the seed integral
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
on the complex
k_{s}
plane.

The strategy is similar to what we adopted in Sec.
3
, namely, to use the contour-deformation method. With this method, we will show that the seed integral
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
is analytic everywhere on the complex
k_{s}
plane, expect for a possible branch cut lying on the whole negative real axis. In the next part, we shall relate the discontinuity of this branch cut to the one in the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
.

Similar to the behavior in the vertex energy plane, the seed integral is obviously analytic in
k_{s}
for
\text{Im}\,k_{s}\neq 0
, a direct consequence of contour deformation argument. More nontrivial is the following fact:

\displaystyle\theta(k_{s})\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=0.

(118)

That is, the seed integral is analytic in
k_{s}
for all
k_{s}>0
. This is quite remarkable because the region
k_{s}>0
is not entirely physical: Physically allowed
k_{s}
satisfies
0\leq k_{s}\leq\text{min}\{k_{12},k_{34}\}
. Thus, the statement (
118
) in particular implies that seed integrals
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}
are regular on the boundaries of the physical region when
k_{s}=k_{12}
and
k_{s}=k_{34}
. As is well known, the absence of singularities at these
folded
configurations is a consequence of choosing the Bunch-Davies initial state for all fluctuating modes in the bulk. Now let us prove (
118
) rigorously using our contour-deformation method.

Once again, the analytical behavior of seed integrals on the complex
k_{s}
plane is governed by the UV behavior of the integrands, namely the convergence of the integral as
\tau_{1,2}\rightarrow-\infty
. So let us look at these UV regions for the opposite-sign integrals
\mathcal{I}_{\pm\mp}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
and same-sign integrals
\mathcal{I}_{\pm\pm}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
, respectively.

First, we consider the opposite-sign integral:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{+-}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{4}k_{s}^{5+p_{12}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\,(-\tau_{1})^{3/2+p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{3/2+p_{2}}

\displaystyle~\times e^{\mathrm{i}k_{12}\tau_{1}-\mathrm{i}k_{34}\tau_{2}}\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-k_{s}\tau_{2}).

(119)

Clearly, the integrand is well defined in the entire integration region for all
k_{s}>0
. Furthermore, we only consider IR finite processes so that the integral is convergent as
\tau_{1,2}\rightarrow 0
. Thus, any potential singularity of the integral on the complex
k_{s}
plane must come from the UV divergences when
\tau_{1,2}\rightarrow-\infty
. However, it is easy to see this never happens for
k_{s}>0
. In fact, using the asymptotic behavior of the Hankel functions (
31
), we see that the integrand behaves like:

e^{+\mathrm{i}(k_{12}+k_{s})\tau_{1}}e^{-\mathrm{i}(k_{34}+k_{s})\tau_{2}},

(120)

up to irrelevant power factors of
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
. We see that, for physical values of
k_{12}
and
k_{34}
, and for any
k_{s}>0
, the phases of these factors never change sign or hit zero. Thus, we conclude that, with the original choices of the integration contour (with proper
\mathrm{i}\epsilon
prescriptions), the opposite-sign integral
\mathcal{I}_{+-}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
is regular for any
k_{s}>0
. With completely the same argument, we can also show that the other opposite-sign integral
\mathcal{I}_{-+}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
is regular for any
k_{s}>0
as well.

The two same-sign seed integrals can be analyzed similarly. Let us consider the all-plus integral:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{++}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{4}k_{s}^{5+p_{12}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\,(-\tau_{1})^{3/2+p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{3/2+p_{2}}e^{\mathrm{i}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}k_{34}\tau_{2}}

\displaystyle~\times\Big[\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-k_{s}\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})+\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-k_{s}\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})\Big].

(121)

Parallel to the previous argument, the integral can develop singular behaviors on the complex
k_{s}
plane only through UV divergences. They occur when either the earlier time variable or both the time variables go to
-\infty
. Taking the
\theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})
part of (
4
) as an example:

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}}\,}{4}k_{s}^{5+p_{12}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{2}\int_{\tau_{2}}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}\,(-\tau_{1})^{3/2+p_{1}}(-\tau_{2})^{3/2+p_{2}}e^{\mathrm{i}k_{12}\tau_{1}+\mathrm{i}k_{34}\tau_{2}}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-k_{s}\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-k_{s}\tau_{2}).

(122)

In the UV limit
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\rightarrow-\infty
, the integrand behaves like
e^{+\mathrm{i}(k_{12}-k_{s})\tau_{1}}e^{+\mathrm{i}(k_{34}+k_{s})\tau_{2}}
up to unimportant power factors. Then, after finishing the
\tau_{1}
integral, we get two terms, each behaves in the
\tau_{2}\rightarrow-\infty
limit as
e^{+\mathrm{i}(k_{12}+k_{34})\tau_{2}}
and
e^{+\mathrm{i}(k_{34}+k_{s})\tau_{2}}
, respectively. Clearly, both phases stay positive for all
k_{s}>0
, so that the integral is well convergent with its original contour. One can similarly analyze the
\theta(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})
term in (
4
) and gets the same result. The analysis for
\mathcal{I}_{--}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
is also the same. Thus we conclude that the same-sign seed integrals are also regular for all
k_{s}>0
. This completes the proof of (
118
).

Discontinuity in the line energy

Figure 9:
The analytical structure of the seed integral
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})
in (
11
) on the complex
k_{s}
plane, with
k_{12}
and
k_{34}
staying in their physical regions
k_{12},k_{34}>0
. The physical region of
k_{s}
(
0\leq k_{s}\leq\text{min}\{k_{12},k_{34}\}
) is marked in green. The seed integral is analytic in
k_{s}
everywhere except when
k_{s}\in(-\infty,0]
. In this interval, we have three branch points: two partial-energy (PE) poles at
k_{s}=-k_{12}
and
k_{s}=-k_{34}
, and the signal branch point at
k_{12}=0
. The total-energy pole
k_{1234}=0
is never met for finite
k_{s}
when
k_{12}\neq-k_{34}
, and can be thought of as sitting at
k_{s}=-\infty
due to the scale invariance of the seed integral. The branch cut can be chosen to be lying in the entire interval
k_{12}\in(-\infty,-k_{s}]
, with the discontinuity itself being discontinuous at the two partial-energy poles.

From (
118
) we see that any possible branch cuts of the seed integrals
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
must lie in the negative real axis. In other words, we have:

\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=\theta(-k_{s})\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})+\mathcal{Q}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})\Big].

(123)

Here we have used (
106
) with all SK indices summed, as well as (
111
), namely, the same-order integral
\mathcal{P}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
with all SK indices summed is nothing but the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\text{NS}}
.

Now we are ready to relates this discontinuity with that of the nonlocal signal. To this end, we make use of the above result (
118
), written in terms of
\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}_{\text{NS}}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{p_{1}p_{2}}
:

\theta(k_{s})\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})+\mathcal{Q}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})\Big]=0.

(124)

Then, using the parity of the opposite-order integral
\mathcal{Q}
in (
117
), we get:

\theta(k_{s})\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})-(-1)^{1+p_{12}}\mathcal{Q}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},-k_{s})\Big]=0.

(125)

This can be equivalently written as:

\theta(-k_{s})\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\mathcal{Q}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=(-1)^{1+p_{12}}\theta(-k_{s})\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},-k_{s}).

(126)

Substituting this relation back to (
123
), we finally get:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\mathcal{I}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})=

\displaystyle~\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{k_{s}}\Big[\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s})-(-1)^{p_{12}}\mathcal{I}_{\text{NS}}^{p_{1}p_{2}}(k_{12},k_{34},-k_{s})\Big]\theta(-k_{s}).

(127)

That is, the discontinuity of the full seed integral can be completely related to that of the nonlocal signal alone. This is the central result of the current section, and forms the basis for the line energy dispersion relation, to be discussed below.

5  Bootstrapping Correlators with Line Dispersion Relation

From the analytical structure of the seed integral on the complex
k_{s}
plane, it is straightforward to construct dispersion integrals, which relate the whole seed integral with its nonlocal signal alone. For clarity, let us still specialize to the case of
p_{1}=p_{2}=-2
. Once again, we use the fact that the dimensionless seed integral depends only on two independent momentum ratios, and we have freedom to choose them. A convenient choice is
r_{1}=k_{s}/k_{12}
and
x\equiv k_{34}/k_{12}
, so that the analytical structure of the seed integral in the line energy
k_{s}
is manifest on the complex
r_{1}
plane. Again, to avoid potential confusions, we introduce a new variable for the seed integral with this particular choice of arguments:

\mathcal{Z}(r_{1}=k_{s}/k_{12},x=k_{34}/k_{12})\equiv\mathcal{I}^{-2,-2}(k_{12},k_{34},k_{s}).

(128)

The integral for the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)
is likewise defined. Then, with this new notation, the discontinuity of the seed integral (
127
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\mathcal{Z}(r_{1},x)=\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\Big[\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)-\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(-r_{1},x)\Big]\theta(-r_{1}),

(129)

where
x
remains in the physical region
x>0
. We show this result in Fig.
10
.

From this result we learn a lesson: The singularity structure of the seed integral as a function of one momentum ratio, say
r_{1}
, is crucially dependent on how we choose and fix other ratios. This is made clear by comparing Fig.
8
, where we fix
r_{2}
, and Fig.
10
, where we fix
x=r_{1}/r_{2}
. There is nothing mysterious here: In the most general situation, the seed integral is to be treated as a function of multiple complex variables, whose singularity structure on a multidimensional complex space can be quite complicated. The dispersion relations considered in this work, on the other hand, are always formulated on a fixed complex dimension-1 submanifold, where we only see the projections of higher dimensional singularities. By fixing different ratios, we are working on different complex dimension-1 submanifolds, and it is not surprising that the projections of singularities on these submanifolds are different.

With the discontinuity given in (
129
), we can directly write down a dispersion integral for the seed integral
\mathcal{Z}(r_{1},x)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(r_{1},x)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}r}\,}{r(r-r_{1})}\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\Big[\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)-\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(-r_{1},x)\Big].

(130)

Here we have introduced a first-order subtraction. This choice follows from the asymptotic behavior of the seed integral
\mathcal{Z}(r_{1},x)
in the limit
|r_{1}|\rightarrow\infty
. Note that
r_{1}=\infty
is the total energy limit where
\mathcal{Z}(r_{1},x)
