diverges at most logarithmically by power counting of time in the time integral. So, a first-order subtraction is sufficient to make the dispersion integral well defined.

Figure 10:
The analytical structure of the 4-point seed integral
\mathcal{Z}(r_{1}^{\prime},x)
on the complex
r_{1}^{\prime}
plane, with
x\in(0,\infty)
fixed in the interior of its physical region. There are three branch points at
r_{1}^{\prime}=0
(signal branch point),
r_{1}^{\prime}=-1
(partial-energy pole
k_{s}=-k_{12}
), and
r_{1}^{\prime}=-x
(partial-energy pole
k_{s}=-k_{34}
), with a branch cut connecting them on the whole negative real axis. In this plot, we also show the insertion of a pole at
r_{1}^{\prime}=r_{1}
at which we compute the seed integral via the line dispersion integral (
130
), whose contour is shown by the blue curve surrounding the branch cut.

The nonlocal signal of the seed integral has been presented in (
113
), and here we rewrite it as a function of
r_{1}
and
x
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1}=k_{s}/k_{12},x=k_{34}/k_{12})=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{4\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\sum_{\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{c}=\pm}\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-2}(\mathsf{a}k_{12},k_{s})\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-2}(\mathsf{b}k_{34},k_{s}),

(131)

Using the result for
\mathcal{V}^{-2}_{\mathsf{c}}
in (
115
), we get an explicit expression for
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{NS}}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{NS}}(r_{1},x)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1-\mathrm{i}\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{2\pi}x^{-1/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1}/x)\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu),

(132)

where
\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{p}
is defined in (
63
).

Discontinuity of the nonlocal signal

To evaluate the dispersion integral (
130
), we need the discontinuity of the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{NS}}
. It is possible to get this discontinuity by analyzing the integral expression for
\mathcal{V}_{\pm}^{p}
without really evaluating it, like we did for
\mathcal{U}_{\pm}^{p}
before. However, here we choose to present the discontinuity directly by known analytical properties of power functions and Gauss’s hypergeometric functions. Notice that the power function
r_{1}^{-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
has a branch cut in the negative real axis, and that the Gauss’s hypergeometric function in
\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}
factors has a branch cut when its argument
z\in(1,\infty)
. Thus, all three
r_{1}
-dependent factors in (
132
) make contributions to the discontinuity of the nonlocal signal. More explicitly:

•

r_{1}^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
contributes a branch cut for
r_{1}\in(-\infty,0)
.

•

\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1}/x)
contributes a branch cut for
r_{1}\in(-\infty,-x)\cup(x,\infty)
.

•

\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1})
contributes a branch cut for
r_{1}\in(-\infty,-1)\cup(1,\infty)
.

Then, it is straightforward to see that the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)
in (
132
) has a branch cut for any real value of
r_{1}
except when
0<r_{1}<\text{min}\{x,1\}
, in which
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)
is real. In addition, the discontinuity across the branch cut is itself discontinuous at
r_{1}=\pm x
and
r_{1}=\pm 1
. We show these branch cuts in Fig.
11
, where we make manifest the contributions from different factors in (
132
).

Figure 11:
The analytical structure of the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)
on the complex
r_{1}
plane, with
x\in(0,\infty)
fixed in the interior of its physical region. The nonlocal signal is discontinuous for all real
r_{1}
except in the physical interval
0<x<\text{min}\{x,1\}
in which
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)
is real. The gray, green, and cyan wiggly lines show the branch cut arising from
r^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
,
\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(\pm r_{1}/x)
, and
\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(\pm r_{1})
, respectively. All branch cuts are on the real axis. However, we offset the green and cyan cuts to highlight our description for computing the discontinuity of a product of functions, and also for clearer illustration.

Incidentally, when we compute a quantity like
\mathop{\text{Disc}}\limits_{z}[f(z)g(z)]
where both
f
and
g
have discontinuities, there are multiple equivalent ways to express it in terms of the discontinuity of individual factor. For instance, when
z>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{z}\Big[f(z)g(z)\Big]=f(z^{+})\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{z}\Big[g(z)\Big]+\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{z}\Big[f(z)\Big]g(z^{-})

\displaystyle=f(z^{-})\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{z}\Big[g(z)\Big]+\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{z}\Big[f(z)\Big]g(z^{+}).

(133)

Here and below, we introduce the shorthand notation
z^{\pm}\equiv ze^{\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon}
with
\epsilon
an infinitesimal positive real. Thus, when computing the discontinuity of products of functions, one can make various choices. To fix our choice, we infinitesimally displace some branch cuts into complex plane, as shown in Fig.
11
. According to this prescription, for instance, when we compute the discontinuity across the cyan branch cut (from
\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1})
factor) in the negative real axis, we should evaluate the other two factors on the lower edges of the gray and green branch cut, namely, we take
(r_{1}^{+})^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
and
\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1}^{+}/x)
.
8
8

8

Note that, for negative
r_{1}
,
r_{1}^{+}
corresponds to the lower edge of the branch cut and
r^{-}
to the upper edge.

The structure of the branch cut suggests that we should break the line dispersion integral (
130
) into three pieces, each corresponding to the branch cut from a given factor, and also to a wiggly line of a given color in Fig.
11
. Below we work out the discontinuity across each of these branch cuts.

First, the gray branch cut in Fig.
11
is contributed by the power factor
r_{1}^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
in (
130
). We define the discontinuity from this branch cut into the following function:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(r_{1},x)\equiv

\displaystyle~\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\Big[r_{1}^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big]\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{-}/x)\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sinh(2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)(-r_{1})^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{-}/x)\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{-}).

(134)

Here the two hypergeometric factors are taking values from the upper edges of their branch cuts on
r_{1}
plane, consistent with our displacement of the branch cuts in Fig.
11
.

Second, the green branch cut in Fig.
11
is contributed by the hypergeometric factor
\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(\pm r_{1}/x)
in (
130
). For negative
r_{1}
, the discontinuity across this branch cut is given by the following function:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(r_{1},x)\equiv

\displaystyle~(r_{1}^{+})^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\Big[\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}/x)\Big]\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-r_{1})^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{G}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}/x)\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1}^{-}),

(135)

where we have defined the discontinuity of
\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(z)
along its branch cut to be
\mathbf{G}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
. Using the known property of the Gauss’s hypergeometric function in (
156
), we can find an explicit expression for
\mathbf{G}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(z)\equiv\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{z}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(z)=-\sqrt{2\pi^{3}}\,\mathrm{csch}\,(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\,{}_{2}\mathrm{F}_{1}\biggl[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{4}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$},\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{4}$}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\\
1\end{matrix}\bigg|1-z^{2}\biggr].~~~~(z<-1)

(136)

Third, the cyan branch cut in Fig.
11
is contributed by the hypergeometric factor
\mathbf{F}_{\pm\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(\pm r_{1})
in (
130
). For negative
r_{1}
, we define the discontinuity across this branch cut into the following function:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(3)}(r_{1},x)\equiv

\displaystyle~(r_{1}^{+})^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{+}/x)\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\Big[\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1})\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(-r_{1})^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\Big[\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{-}/x)-\mathbf{G}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}/x)\Big]\mathbf{G}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}),

(137)

where we have used the relation
\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{+}/x)=\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}^{-}/x)+\mathbf{G}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}/x)
to rewrite a hypergeometric factor in terms of its value across the branch cut.

In summary, for negative values of
r_{1}
, the discontinuity across the branch cuts of the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)
is given by:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1}<0,x)=\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(r_{1},x)\theta(-r_{1})+\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(r_{1},x)\theta(-r_{1}-x)+\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(3)}(r_{1},x)\theta(-r_{1}-1).

(138)

On the other hand, as shown in Fig.
11
, the green and cyan branch cuts also extend to positive real values of
r_{1}
. However, the discontinuities for these “positive” branch cuts are not independent, since the two “positive” branch cuts can be related to the corresponding two “negative” branch cuts by a
180^{\circ}
rotation around the origin
r_{1}=0
via the
lower
plane (in order not to cross the gray branch cut). Now, using the facts that the function
\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1})
is even in
r_{1}
, and that
(e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi}r_{1})^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}=-e^{-2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
for
r_{1}>0
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Disc}}_{r_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1}>0,x)=e^{2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(r_{1},x)\theta(r_{1}-x)+e^{2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(3)}(r_{1},x)\theta(r_{1}-1).

(139)

Thus we have found the explicit expressions for all five branch cuts shown in Fig.
11
.

Line dispersion integral and the result

Based on the previous analysis of the branch cut of the nonlocal signal, we are now ready to find the explicit expression for the line dispersion integral. Combining (
138
) and (
139
), we see that the dispersion integral (
130
) now boils down to three integrals
\mathbf{J}_{i}^{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}

(i=1,2,3)
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}(r_{1},x)=-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}+\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{4\pi^{2}}x^{-1/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{r_{1}}\Bigl[\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(r_{1},x)+\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(r_{1},x)+\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(3)}(r_{1},x)\Bigr]+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu),

(140)

where the three terms
(\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)},\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)},\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(3)})
correspond to integrals around the gray, green, and cyan branch cuts in Fig.
11
, respectively:

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(r_{1},x)\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}r\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(r,x)}\,}{r(r-r_{1})},

(141)

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(r_{1},x)\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{-x}{\mathrm{d}}r\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1-e^{2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu})\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(r,x)}\,}{r(r-r_{1})},

(142)

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(3)}(r_{1},x)\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}r\displaystyle\frac{\,{(1-e^{2\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu})\mathbf{D}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(3)}(r,x)}\,}{r(r-r_{1})}.

(143)

These three integrals can be computed analytically via PMB representation, although the details are quite lengthy. We collect them in App.
D
, and present the results below.

As mentioned many times before, the 4-point seed integral
\mathcal{Z}(r_{1},x)
can be written as a sum of nonlocal signal (NS), the local signal (LS), and the background (BG):

\mathcal{Z}(r_{1},x)=\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}(r_{1},x)+\mathcal{Z}_{\text{LS}}(r_{1},x)+\mathcal{Z}_{\text{BG}}(r_{1},x).

(144)

It turns out that the nonlocal signal
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{NS}}
is contributed only by the integral around the gray branch cut, namely
\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}
. The result is simply identical to our input (
132
), which we collect here for completeness:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{NS}}(r_{1},x)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1-\mathrm{i}\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{2\pi}x^{-1/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}^{1-2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1}/x)\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu),

(145)

The local signal
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{LS}}
receives contributions from the integrals around the gray and the green branch cuts, namely
\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}
, respectively. The result is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{LS}}(r_{1},x)=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{LS},>}(r_{1},x)\theta\big(1-|x|\big)+\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{LS},>}(r_{1}/x,1/x)\theta\big(|x|-1\big),

(146)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{LS},>}(r_{1},x)=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{1-\mathrm{i}\sinh(\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{2\pi}x^{-1/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}r_{1}\mathbf{F}^{-2}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(r_{1}/x)\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{-2}(r_{1})+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu).

(147)

Finally, the background
\mathcal{Z}_{\text{BG}}
receives contributions from the integrals around all three branch cuts, namely
\mathbf{J}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(i)}

(i=1,2,3)
, whose result can be simplified into the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{BG}}(r_{1},x)=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{BG},>}(r_{1},x)\theta\big(1-|x|\big)+\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{BG},>}(r_{1}/x,1/x)\theta\big(|x|-1\big),

(148)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{BG},>}(r_{1},x)=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{8(-x)^{n}r_{1}}{(1+2n)^{2}+4\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}\,{}_{3}\mathrm{F}_{2}\biggl[\begin{matrix}1,\frac{1}{2}+\frac{n}{2},1+\frac{n}{2}\\
\frac{5}{4}+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{2},\frac{5}{4}+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{2}\end{matrix}\bigg|r_{1}^{2}\biggr].

(149)

This expression for
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{BG}}
has a different look from known results in
[
39
]
, but is identical to the latter. In fact, the background part is a two-variable hypergeometric function known as Kampé de Fériet function and allows for many different series representations
[
7
,
39
,
42
]
.

6  Conclusions and Outlooks

As the dS counterparts of flat-space scattering amplitudes, inflation correlators possess distinct analytical structure. For general massive-exchange processes, branch cuts usually appear in the complex plane of appropriate kinematic variables, which connect physics in the UV and IR regions. In the IR regions, such branch cuts are closely related to logarithmic oscillations of the correlators in the physical regions, known as CC signals in the context of Cosmological Collider physics. The CC signals have received many studies in recent years. In particular, it has been shown that, although the computation of general inflation correlators is difficult, we can apply the cutting rule and the factorization theorem to extract CC signals in the squeezed limit
[
33
,
49
,
50
]
. In comparison, the corresponding branch cuts on the complex domain beyond the physical region are less understood and deserve more studies.

In this work, we explore the analytical properties of massive inflation correlators as functions of two types of kinematic variables: the vertex energies and the line energies. For both types of energies of a tree-level correlator, we identified the total-energy and partial-energy poles, the signal branch point, together with the branch cuts connecting them. Based on this structure, we developed two distinct dispersion relations: a vertex dispersion relation which relates the correlator to its full signal, and a line dispersion relation which relates the correlator to the nonlocal signal alone. With these dispersion relations, we have successfully bootstrapped a few tree-level and 1-loop massive inflation correlators. At 1-loop level, our method is manifestly UV finite and free from any regularization procedure. This allows us to neatly single out the renormalization-independent part of the correlator, which is unambiguously determined by analyticity.

Although there have been scattered studies on analytical properties of inflation correlators (and the related wavefunction coefficients), to our best knowledge, the dispersion relations have not been used to bootstrap the full massive inflation correlators. Our work filled this gap by providing a few proof-of-principle calculations. While the computation itself can often become lengthy compared to other existing methods for simple examples, it nevertheless shows the potential power of the dispersion techniques in bootstrapping more complicated diagrams. Thus, we consider this work a first step in carrying out a more extensive program of dispersive bootstrap. Naturally, many directions are open to further explorations, and we conclude this work by mentioning some of them.

A natural first task is to chart all nonanalyticities of a given tree diagram, beyond the 4-point single exchange. This includes not only the locations of poles and branch cuts, but also the discontinuities across all branch cuts. With these data, we can imagine to recursively bootstrap more complicated diagrams from simple sub-diagrams, either analytically or numerically.

Next, it would be very interesting to explore the potential of dispersive bootstrap for loop diagrams. We have seen that dispersion relations could be advantageous in bootstrapping one-loop diagrams, including the absence of UV divergences, the simplified expressions, and the separation of renormalization dependent and independent parts. They encourage us to consider more complicated loop processes. As a concrete first step, we may try to combine the dispersive and spectral methods and bootstrap 1-loop bubble processes with spinning exchanges and derivative couplings, and this will be explored in a follow-up work. Beyond the bubble topology, it is not immediately clear that techniques like spectral decomposition are still available. Nevertheless, it looks promising to us to numerically implement the dispersive techniques for loop processes. We plan to investigate this route in a future work.

As we pointed out many times, the dispersive bootstrap at its core is an idea to reconstruct the whole diagram from a knowledge of sub-diagrams. In this regard, what we have considered in this work is the most straightforward realization, namely, exploiting the complex energy planes. It is also interesting to search for “dispersion relations” with not only complex energies, but also other complex parameters. In flat space, it has been very fruitful to consider scattering amplitudes on complex planes of mass, angular momentum, and even spacetime dimensions. We can imagine that the analytical structures in these complex parameters could also bring us new insights and new methods for inflation correlators. Also, it has been shown recently that the parity-odd part of a cosmological correlator (or a wavefunction coefficient) automatically factorized under rather general conditions.
[
104
,
40
,
55
,
71
]
Thus, it would be very interesting to develop dispersion techniques for parity-violating theories.

Last but not least, the dispersion relations in flat spacetime or in CFT are usually tied to nonperturbative properties of amplitudes, and are used to make nonperturbative statements about the unitarity and positivity of the theory. On the other hand, in this work, we only apply the dispersion techniques at the diagrammatic level. How are the two approaches related? From pure diagrammatic analysis, is it possible to gain insights applicable to all orders in perturbation theory? Similar to flat-space situations, we believe that, at least for simple kinematics with full dS isometries, it is possible to make progress along these directions. We leave all these interesting topics for future studies.
